People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, I'm here because my family—specifically my daughter, she's the one who made the appointment—thinks I'm overdoing it. They say I've been doing too much, but honestly, it just feels like I'm the only one with enough drive to actually get things done around here. We're in the middle of this big move, you see, downsizing to a smaller place, and there are boxes everywhere. My wife just stares at them, but I've been up sorting, packing, labeling—color-coded labels, mind you, so we know exactly what goes where. 

And then there's the new house. The garage there is a disaster, absolute disaster. I've been drawing up blueprints to rebuild the whole shelving system because the previous owner had no idea what he was doing. I need a proper space for my tools, especially the grinding gear I sometimes bring home from the shop. 

On top of all that, I’m organizing the annual charity drive at church. Pastor Jenkins is a good man, but he’s slow, you know? He needs someone to take the reins, so I’ve been calling the volunteers, setting up the schedules, mapping out the donation routes. And I'm still working full shifts at the plant. I’m a mixing and blending worker, have been for years, and these younger fellows they hire now, they just don't have the work ethic. I have to show them, step-by-step, how the job ought to be done, otherwise the whole batch gets ruined. So, yes, I'm busy, very busy, but someone has to do it, right? If I don't, it all falls apart. But my daughter, she just doesn't see it that way. She thinks I need to "slow down." Slow down! Can you imagine? At 74, I've got more energy than I did at 40!

Well, energetic is one word for it, doctor, but it’s more like... it’s like my whole body is just humming, you know? Like I’ve got a motor in me that just won’t shut off. I mean, take yesterday for example. I tried, I really tried to sit down in the recliner—the brown leather one, my wife bought it for my birthday three years ago because she said I needed a place to relax—but I couldn't even make it through five minutes of the evening news. The anchor was just droning on about the weather, and all I could think about was the fact that the garage at the new place still hasn't been measured properly for those shelving blueprints I told you about. 

So, I’m up out of the chair, pacing around, sorting through the boxes in the living room—the ones with the red labels, those are for the kitchen appliances, obviously—and then I’m out in the driveway loading my tools into the truck. I had my socket wrenches, the power drill, the grinding gear I brought from the plant—because you never know when you're going to need to smooth down a rough edge on a bracket—and before I know it, I’m driving back and forth between the old house and the new house. I just can't stop moving. It’s like if I stop, everything is going to pile up, and nobody else is going to handle it. My wife just watches me, shaking her head, but I tell her, "Martha, the boxes aren't going to unpack themselves, and the shelves aren't going to build themselves." So, yes, the energy is there, constantly, pushing me from one task to the next, hour after hour, and I just don't have the time to sit still when there's so much left to do.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, "top of the world" is exactly how I'd put it. I mean, I wake up and I just feel... magnificent. Like I'm ten feet tall! I'll go into the plant and I'm joking with the guys on the floor, laughing, feeling like I could run the entire blending operation single-handed. But the problem—and this is what my daughter doesn't seem to understand—the problem isn't me, it's everybody else. 

I’ve just been feeling lately like everybody needs to either get on board with my plans or get completely out of my way. Because I have a vision for how things need to be done, whether it's the church donation routes or the packing system for the new house, and they all just drag their feet. So, yes, I suppose I do get annoyed, but I wouldn't say it's for "no clear reason." I’ll go from joking around and feeling absolutely invincible to just snapping at my wife or my boy over some little question. 

Like yesterday, my son came over to help with the heavy boxes—the blue-labeled ones, obviously for the basement—and he actually asked me if we should just leave them in the hallway for now. The hallway! After I had just spent three hours drawing up a precise floor plan for the basement storage! Or Martha, she came out to the driveway and asked me what I wanted for lunch right when I was in the middle of calculating the load-bearing capacity for the new shelving brackets. Just a little question, I know, but I completely lost it. I snapped at her. I said, "Martha, can't you see I'm doing the work of ten men here?" It's just... they interrupt me with these tiny, insignificant things when I'm trying to accomplish something massive, and it just sets me right off. So, high mood? Absolutely. But irritable? Only because nobody else has the sense to keep up with the program!

The anchor was just droning on about the weather, and all I could think about was the fact that the garage at the new place still hasn't been measured properly for those shelving blueprints I told you about. \n\nSo, I’m up out of the chair, pacing around, sorting through the boxes in the living room—the ones with the red labels, those are for the kitchen appliances, obviously—and then I’m out in the driveway loading my tools into the truck. I just can't stop moving. It’s like if I stop, everything is going to pile up, and nobody else is going to handle it. But the problem—and this is what my daughter doesn't seem to understand—the problem isn't me, it's everybody else. \n\nI’ve just been feeling lately like everybody needs to either get on board with my plans or get completely out of my way. Because I have a vision for how things need to be done, whether it's the church donation routes or the packing system for the new house, and they all just drag their feet. The hallway! After I had just spent three hours drawing up a precise floor plan for the basement storage! Or Martha, she came out to the driveway and asked me what I wanted for lunch right when I was in the middle of calculating the load-bearing capacity for the new shelving brackets. Just a little question, I know, but I completely lost it. I snapped at her. I said, \"Martha, can't you see I'm doing the work of ten men here?\" It's just... they interrupt me with these tiny, insignificant things when I'm trying to accomplish something massive, and it just sets me right off. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, sleep... sleep is an interesting topic right now, doctor. You see, I used to be a solid eight-hours-a-night man, absolutely had to have it, especially working the early shifts at the plant. You can't be nodding off when you're calibrating the grinding gear, the tolerances are just too tight. But lately? Honestly, I don't know what to tell you. I haven't really been paying attention to the clock at night. 

I mean, take last night. Martha went up to bed around, oh, maybe ten-thirty? She always watches that crime show and then goes up. I stayed down because I was right in the middle of re-evaluating the schedule for the church charity drive. I had all these spreadsheets spread out across the dining room table—which, by the way, is a logistical nightmare because half the table is covered in those green-labeled boxes for the den—and I just kept working. I was cross-referencing the volunteer availability with the donation drop-off points, and the next thing I knew, I looked out the window and the sun was coming up over the Henderson's roof across the street! 

But the funny thing is, I didn't feel tired. Not even a little bit! I just made a pot of coffee—the strong stuff, French roast—and went straight out to the driveway to finish sorting the socket wrenches before I had to leave for my shift at the plant. So, do I need less sleep? I'm not really sure if I *need* less, or if I just don't have the time for it right now. There's just so much to do, and my mind is so sharp, I don't even feel the lack of it. I just keep going and going. I haven't really noticed feeling exhausted or anything like that the next day. I'm just... I'm just ready for the next task!

I have to show them, step-by-step, how the job ought to be done, otherwise the whole batch gets ruined. So, yes, I'm busy, very busy, but someone has to do it, right? If I don't, it all falls apart. But my daughter, she just doesn't see it that way. She thinks I need to \"slow down.\" Slow down! Can you imagine? At 74, I've got more energy than I did at 40!\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of time where you felt unusually energetic, needed less sleep, or were more talkative than usual, lasting for several days or more?\n\nPatient: Well, energetic is one word for it, doctor, but it’s more like... it’s like my whole body is just humming, you know? Like I’ve got a motor in me that just won’t shut off. I mean, take yesterday for example. I tried, I really tried to sit down in the recliner—the brown leather one, my wife bought it for my birthday three years ago because she said I needed a place to relax—but I couldn't even make it through five minutes of the evening news. The anchor was just droning on about the weather, and all I could think about was the fact that the garage at the new place still hasn't been measured properly for those shelving blueprints I told you about. \n\nSo, I’m up out of the chair, pacing around, sorting through the boxes in the living room—the ones with the red labels, those are for the kitchen appliances, obviously—and then I’m out in the driveway loading my tools into the truck. I had my socket wrenches, the power drill, the grinding gear I brought from the plant—because you never know when you're going to need to smooth down a rough edge on a bracket—and before I know it, I’m driving back and forth between the old house and the new house. I just can't stop moving. It’s like if I stop, everything is going to pile up, and nobody else is going to handle it. My wife just watches me, shaking her head, but I tell her, \"Martha, the boxes aren't going to unpack themselves, and the shelves aren't going to build themselves.\" So, yes, the energy is there, constantly, pushing me from one task to the next, hour after hour, and I just don't have the time to sit still when there's so much left to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your mood was unusually high, euphoric, or irritable—where you felt like you were on top of the world or easily annoyed for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, \"top of the world\" is exactly how I'd put it. I mean, I wake up and I just feel... magnificent. Like I'm ten feet tall! I'll go into the plant and I'm joking with the guys on the floor, laughing, feeling like I could run the entire blending operation single-handed. But the problem—and this is what my daughter doesn't seem to understand—the problem isn't me, it's everybody else. \n\nI’ve just been feeling lately like everybody needs to either get on board with my plans or get completely out of my way. Because I have a vision for how things need to be done, whether it's the church donation routes or the packing system for the new house, and they all just drag their feet. So, yes, I suppose I do get annoyed, but I wouldn't say it's for \"no clear reason.\" I’ll go from joking around and feeling absolutely invincible to just snapping at my wife or my boy over some little question. \n\nLike yesterday, my son came over to help with the heavy boxes—the blue-labeled ones, obviously for the basement—and he actually asked me if we should just leave them in the hallway for now. The hallway! After I had just spent three hours drawing up a precise floor plan for the basement storage! Or Martha, she came out to the driveway and asked me what I wanted for lunch right when I was in the middle of calculating the load-bearing capacity for the new shelving brackets. Just a little question, I know, but I completely lost it. I snapped at her. I said, \"Martha, can't you see I'm doing the work of ten men here?\" It's just... they interrupt me with these tiny, insignificant things when I'm trying to accomplish something massive, and it just sets me right off. So, high mood? Absolutely. But irritable? Only because nobody else has the sense to keep up with the program!\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you noticed that you need much less sleep lately—like feeling rested after only a few hours of sleep—without feeling tired the next day?\n\nPatient: Well, sleep... sleep is an interesting topic right now, doctor. You can't be nodding off when you're calibrating the grinding gear, the tolerances are just too tight. But lately? Honestly, I don't know what to tell you. I haven't really been paying attention to the clock at night. \n\nI mean, take last night. Martha went up to bed around, oh, maybe ten-thirty? She always watches that crime show and then goes up. I stayed down because I was right in the middle of re-evaluating the schedule for the church charity drive. I had all these spreadsheets spread out across the dining room table—which, by the way, is a logistical nightmare because half the table is covered in those green-labeled boxes for the den—and I just kept working. I was cross-referencing the volunteer availability with the donation drop-off points, and the next thing I knew, I looked out the window and the sun was coming up over the Henderson's roof across the street! \n\nBut the funny thing is, I didn't feel tired. Not even a little bit! I just made a pot of coffee—the strong stuff, French roast—and went straight out to the driveway to finish sorting the socket wrenches before I had to leave for my shift at the plant. The anchor was just droning on about the weather, and all I could think about was the fact that the garage at the new place still hasn't been measured properly for those shelving blueprints I told you about. \n\nSo, I’m up out of the chair, pacing around, sorting through the boxes in the living room—the ones with the red labels, those are for the kitchen appliances, obviously—and then I’m out in the driveway loading my tools into the truck. I had my socket wrenches, the power drill, the grinding gear I brought from the plant—because you never know when you're going to need to smooth down a rough edge on a bracket—and before I know it, I’m driving back and forth between the old house and the new house. I just can't stop moving. It’s like if I stop, everything is going to pile up, and nobody else is going to handle it. My wife just watches me, shaking her head, but I tell her, \"Martha, the boxes aren't going to unpack themselves, and the shelves aren't going to build themselves.\" So, yes, the energy is there, constantly, pushing me from one task to the next, hour after hour, and I just don't have the time to sit still when there's so much left to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your mood was unusually high, euphoric, or irritable—where you felt like you were on top of the world or easily annoyed for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, \"top of the world\" is exactly how I'd put it. I mean, I wake up and I just feel... magnificent. Like I'm ten feet tall! I'll go into the plant and I'm joking with the guys on the floor, laughing, feeling like I could run the entire blending operation single-handed. But the problem—and this is what my daughter doesn't seem to understand—the problem isn't me, it's everybody else. \n\nI’ve just been feeling lately like everybody needs to either get on board with my plans or get completely out of my way. Because I have a vision for how things need to be done, whether it's the church donation routes or the packing system for the new house, and they all just drag their feet. So, yes, I suppose I do get annoyed, but I wouldn't say it's for \"no clear reason.\" I’ll go from joking around and feeling absolutely invincible to just snapping at my wife or my boy over some little question. \n\nLike yesterday, my son came over to help with the heavy boxes—the blue-labeled ones, obviously for the basement—and he actually asked me if we should just leave them in the hallway for now. The hallway! After I had just spent three hours drawing up a precise floor plan for the basement storage! Or Martha, she came out to the driveway and asked me what I wanted for lunch right when I was in the middle of calculating the load-bearing capacity for the new shelving brackets. Just a little question, I know, but I completely lost it. I snapped at her. I said, \"Martha, can't you see I'm doing the work of ten men here?\" It's just... they interrupt me with these tiny, insignificant things when I'm trying to accomplish something massive, and it just sets me right off. So, high mood? Absolutely. But irritable? Only because nobody else has the sense to keep up with the program!\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you noticed that you need much less sleep lately—like feeling rested after only a few hours of sleep—without feeling tired the next day?\n\nPatient: Well, sleep... sleep is an interesting topic right now, doctor. You see, I used to be a solid eight-hours-a-night man, absolutely had to have it, especially working the early shifts at the plant. You can't be nodding off when you're calibrating the grinding gear, the tolerances are just too tight. But lately? Honestly, I don't know what to tell you. I haven't really been paying attention to the clock at night. \n\nI mean, take last night. Martha went up to bed around, oh, maybe ten-thirty? She always watches that crime show and then goes up. I stayed down because I was right in the middle of re-evaluating the schedule for the church charity drive. I had all these spreadsheets spread out across the dining room table—which, by the way, is a logistical nightmare because half the table is covered in those green-labeled boxes for the den—and I just kept working. I was cross-referencing the volunteer availability with the donation drop-off points, and the next thing I knew, I looked out the window and the sun was coming up over the Henderson's roof across the street! \n\nBut the funny thing is, I didn't feel tired. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Distracted? Well, doctor, I wouldn't say distracted so much as... highly attuned to my environment. My senses are just razor-sharp right now! But, yes, Martha has been harping on this exact thing, and I suppose if you look at it from the outside, I can see how she might think I'm taking on too much. 

It’s like this—whether I’m down at the shop or working on the house, absolutely everything catches my attention. I’ll be right in the middle of a crucial task, say, adjusting the torque on the main grinding wheel at the plant, and then—*clank*—I hear a noise over by the loading dock. Or a delivery truck passes by outside, and I just have to go see if it's the new shipment of abrasive belts we ordered last Tuesday. And on the way to the loading dock, I’ll spot one tool out of place on Jimmy’s workbench—Jimmy never puts his calipers away right, I’ve told him a hundred times—so I stop to organize his station. But while I'm doing that, I realize the lighting over his bench is completely inadequate, so I head to the supply closet to find a higher wattage bulb. Just one little thing pulls me right off what I’m doing!

And it's the exact same story at home with the packing. One minute I'm taping up those green-labeled boxes for the den, the next I hear the mail truck, so I go out to check the mail, notice the hinges on the mailbox are squeaking, head to the garage for the oil, and then I decide the whole garage workbench needs to be reorganized right that second based on those blueprints I was telling you about. Pretty soon, I’ve got five different things started—the packing, the mail, the hinges, the workbench, the lighting—and none of them are actually finished! Martha gets so frustrated, but I try to explain to her that they all need doing, every single one of them. It's just that my brain is moving so fast, I'm trying to tackle the whole world all at once!

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Risky? Impulsive? Well, doctor, that depends entirely on who you ask. If you ask Martha, she’ll tell you I’ve completely lost my sense of reason, but if you ask me, I call it being decisive. You see, I was telling you earlier about the garage at the new house and those blueprints I drew up. Well, you can't execute a professional-grade organizational system on a slanted concrete floor with a bunch of rusted-out pegboards. It’s impossible! So I went down to the supply center—and I also called up this contractor, Hank, who I actually took a couple of business management courses with at the community college back in the late seventies, good guy—and I just went ahead and put the deposits down. Right then and there! I put down money for garage work, a commercial air compressor, custom steel cabinetry, the whole nine yards. 

And yes, I did it without talking it over with my wife. But doctor, if I wait for Martha to sit down at the kitchen table, balance the checkbook, and deliberate over every single penny, that garage wouldn't be ready until next Christmas! I had to pull the trigger! The equipment was exactly what my setup needed.

And because I’m trying to coordinate all this, I’ve been driving back and forth between the old house and the new house. Just back and forth, back and forth, probably six or seven times a day. Martha's sister rode with me once and practically left fingernail marks in the dashboard, but I'm just trying to get things done! And it’s the exact same story down at the plant. I’ve been running the blending vats and the grinders at absolute maximum capacity, pushing the mixing cycles through in record time because I know the machines can handle it. Dave, the floor manager, actually pulled me aside and told me I needed to slow down. He and a few others kept warning me, saying I was taking chances, that I was rushing the jobs and pushing the equipment too hard. 

So, sure, people are warning me. They’re telling me I’m taking risks on the road, taking risks at work, spending money... but they just don’t get the big picture! When your mind is firing this fast and you can see exactly how everything needs to come together, you can't just sit around in the slow lane waiting for the rest of the world to catch up!

Pastor Jenkins is a good man, but he’s slow, you know? He needs someone to take the reins, so I’ve been calling the volunteers, setting up the schedules, mapping out the donation routes. I have to show them, step-by-step, how the job ought to be done, otherwise the whole batch gets ruined. So, yes, I'm busy, very busy, but someone has to do it, right? If I don't, it all falls apart. Like I’ve got a motor in me that just won’t shut off. I mean, take yesterday for example. I just can't stop moving. It’s like if I stop, everything is going to pile up, and nobody else is going to handle it. My wife just watches me, shaking her head, but I tell her, \"Martha, the boxes aren't going to unpack themselves, and the shelves aren't going to build themselves.\" So, yes, the energy is there, constantly, pushing me from one task to the next, hour after hour, and I just don't have the time to sit still when there's so much left to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your mood was unusually high, euphoric, or irritable—where you felt like you were on top of the world or easily annoyed for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, \"top of the world\" is exactly how I'd put it. I mean, I wake up and I just feel... magnificent. Like I'm ten feet tall! I'll go into the plant and I'm joking with the guys on the floor, laughing, feeling like I could run the entire blending operation single-handed. So, yes, I suppose I do get annoyed, but I wouldn't say it's for \"no clear reason.\" I’ll go from joking around and feeling absolutely invincible to just snapping at my wife or my boy over some little question. \n\nLike yesterday, my son came over to help with the heavy boxes—the blue-labeled ones, obviously for the basement—and he actually asked me if we should just leave them in the hallway for now. The hallway! After I had just spent three hours drawing up a precise floor plan for the basement storage! Or Martha, she came out to the driveway and asked me what I wanted for lunch right when I was in the middle of calculating the load-bearing capacity for the new shelving brackets. Just a little question, I know, but I completely lost it. I snapped at her. I said, \"Martha, can't you see I'm doing the work of ten men here?\" It's just... they interrupt me with these tiny, insignificant things when I'm trying to accomplish something massive, and it just sets me right off. So, high mood? Absolutely. But irritable? Only because nobody else has the sense to keep up with the program!\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you noticed that you need much less sleep lately—like feeling rested after only a few hours of sleep—without feeling tired the next day?\n\nPatient: Well, sleep... sleep is an interesting topic right now, doctor. You see, I used to be a solid eight-hours-a-night man, absolutely had to have it, especially working the early shifts at the plant. You can't be nodding off when you're calibrating the grinding gear, the tolerances are just too tight. But lately? Honestly, I don't know what to tell you. I haven't really been paying attention to the clock at night. \n\nI mean, take last night. Martha went up to bed around, oh, maybe ten-thirty? She always watches that crime show and then goes up. I stayed down because I was right in the middle of re-evaluating the schedule for the church charity drive. I had all these spreadsheets spread out across the dining room table—which, by the way, is a logistical nightmare because half the table is covered in those green-labeled boxes for the den—and I just kept working. I was cross-referencing the volunteer availability with the donation drop-off points, and the next thing I knew, I looked out the window and the sun was coming up over the Henderson's roof across the street! \n\nBut the funny thing is, I didn't feel tired. Not even a little bit! I just made a pot of coffee—the strong stuff, French roast—and went straight out to the driveway to finish sorting the socket wrenches before I had to leave for my shift at the plant. So, do I need less sleep? I'm not really sure if I *need* less, or if I just don't have the time for it right now. There's just so much to do, and my mind is so sharp, I don't even feel the lack of it. I just keep going and going. I haven't really noticed feeling exhausted or anything like that the next day. I'm just... I'm just ready for the next task!\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself getting easily distracted by things around you, or have you been taking on more tasks or projects than you normally would, even if they seem unnecessary?\n\nPatient: Distracted? Well, doctor, I wouldn't say distracted so much as... highly attuned to my environment. My senses are just razor-sharp right now! But, yes, Martha has been harping on this exact thing, and I suppose if you look at it from the outside, I can see how she might think I'm taking on too much. \n\nIt’s like this—whether I’m down at the shop or working on the house, absolutely everything catches my attention. I’ll be right in the middle of a crucial task, say, adjusting the torque on the main grinding wheel at the plant, and then—*clank*—I hear a noise over by the loading dock. Or a delivery truck passes by outside, and I just have to go see if it's the new shipment of abrasive belts we ordered last Tuesday. And on the way to the loading dock, I’ll spot one tool out of place on Jimmy’s workbench—Jimmy never puts his calipers away right, I’ve told him a hundred times—so I stop to organize his station. But while I'm doing that, I realize the lighting over his bench is completely inadequate, so I head to the supply closet to find a higher wattage bulb. Just one little thing pulls me right off what I’m doing!\n\nAnd it's the exact same story at home with the packing. One minute I'm taping up those green-labeled boxes for the den, the next I hear the mail truck, so I go out to check the mail, notice the hinges on the mailbox are squeaking, head to the garage for the oil, and then I decide the whole garage workbench needs to be reorganized right that second based on those blueprints I was telling you about. Pretty soon, I’ve got five different things started—the packing, the mail, the hinges, the workbench, the lighting—and none of them are actually finished! Martha gets so frustrated, but I try to explain to her that they all need doing, every single one of them. It's just that my brain is moving so fast, I'm trying to tackle the whole world all at once!\n\nDoctor: Have you recently engaged in any activities that others might consider risky, impulsive, or out of character for you—like spending sprees, reckless driving, or making major decisions without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Risky? Impulsive? Well, doctor, that depends entirely on who you ask. If you ask Martha, she’ll tell you I’ve completely lost my sense of reason, but if you ask me, I call it being decisive. You see, I was telling you earlier about the garage at the new house and those blueprints I drew up. Well, you can't execute a professional-grade organizational system on a slanted concrete floor with a bunch of rusted-out pegboards. It’s impossible! So I went down to the supply center—and I also called up this contractor, Hank, who I actually took a couple of business management courses with at the community college back in the late seventies, good guy—and I just went ahead and put the deposits down. Right then and there! I put down money for garage work, a commercial air compressor, custom steel cabinetry, the whole nine yards. \n\nAnd yes, I did it without talking it over with my wife. But doctor, if I wait for Martha to sit down at the kitchen table, balance the checkbook, and deliberate over every single penny, that garage wouldn't be ready until next Christmas! I had to pull the trigger! The equipment was exactly what my setup needed.\n\nAnd because I’m trying to coordinate all this, I’ve been driving back and forth between the old house and the new house. Just back and forth, back and forth, probably six or seven times a day. Martha's sister rode with me once and practically left fingernail marks in the dashboard, but I'm just trying to get things done! And it’s the exact same story down at the plant. I’ve been running the blending vats and the grinders at absolute maximum capacity, pushing the mixing cycles through in record time because I know the machines can handle it. Dave, the floor manager, actually pulled me aside and told me I needed to slow down. He and a few others kept warning me, saying I was taking chances, that I was rushing the jobs and pushing the equipment too hard. \n\nSo, sure, people are warning me. They’re telling me I’m taking risks on the road, taking risks at work, spending money... but they just don’t get the big picture! When your mind is firing this fast and you can see exactly how everything needs to come together, you can't just sit around in the slow lane waiting for the rest of the world to catch up!\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Exactly how long? Well, doctor, if you want the exact timeline, you have to look at what set this whole massive operation into motion. It all really kicked into high gear right when we were forced to start leaving the old house. We didn't want to move, you know. Thirty-two years in that house! My tomatoes in the back garden were legendary in the neighborhood. And then the county comes in with their zoning changes and eminent domain nonsense for the new highway bypass, and suddenly we are literally being forced out. Can you imagine the sheer audacity of it? Just uprooting a man's entire life! 

Martha was an absolute wreck about it, just paralyzed, sitting there with a roll of packing tape in her lap, crying and looking at the walls. But me? I realized right then and there that somebody had to take the bull by the horns. You can't just sit around and let the county dictate your momentum! So, that was... let me think, the final notices came in late summer, but the actual day we had to start physically leaving, getting our stuff out and dealing with the new property? That was... well, it's been about a month now. 

Yes, a solid month. For four weeks, thirty days straight, I have just been riding this incredible wave of momentum. Ever since we were forced out and that very first box got packed a month ago, my mind just snapped to attention. I saw all the logistics, the garage blueprints, the scheduling for the plant, the church drive—everything just clicked into perfect clarity the minute we had to start leaving that house. So it's been about a month of just absolute, pure productivity, and honestly, I don't see any reason to slow down now!

The anchor was just droning on about the weather, and all I could think about was the fact that the garage at the new place still hasn't been measured properly for those shelving blueprints I told you about. \n\nSo, I’m up out of the chair, pacing around, sorting through the boxes in the living room—the ones with the red labels, those are for the kitchen appliances, obviously—and then I’m out in the driveway loading my tools into the truck. I had my socket wrenches, the power drill, the grinding gear I brought from the plant—because you never know when you're going to need to smooth down a rough edge on a bracket—and before I know it, I’m driving back and forth between the old house and the new house. I just can't stop moving. It’s like if I stop, everything is going to pile up, and nobody else is going to handle it. My wife just watches me, shaking her head, but I tell her, \"Martha, the boxes aren't going to unpack themselves, and the shelves aren't going to build themselves.\" So, yes, the energy is there, constantly, pushing me from one task to the next, hour after hour, and I just don't have the time to sit still when there's so much left to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your mood was unusually high, euphoric, or irritable—where you felt like you were on top of the world or easily annoyed for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, \"top of the world\" is exactly how I'd put it. I mean, I wake up and I just feel... magnificent. Like I'm ten feet tall! I'll go into the plant and I'm joking with the guys on the floor, laughing, feeling like I could run the entire blending operation single-handed. But the problem—and this is what my daughter doesn't seem to understand—the problem isn't me, it's everybody else. \n\nI’ve just been feeling lately like everybody needs to either get on board with my plans or get completely out of my way. Because I have a vision for how things need to be done, whether it's the church donation routes or the packing system for the new house, and they all just drag their feet. So, yes, I suppose I do get annoyed, but I wouldn't say it's for \"no clear reason.\" I’ll go from joking around and feeling absolutely invincible to just snapping at my wife or my boy over some little question. \n\nLike yesterday, my son came over to help with the heavy boxes—the blue-labeled ones, obviously for the basement—and he actually asked me if we should just leave them in the hallway for now. The hallway! After I had just spent three hours drawing up a precise floor plan for the basement storage! Or Martha, she came out to the driveway and asked me what I wanted for lunch right when I was in the middle of calculating the load-bearing capacity for the new shelving brackets. Just a little question, I know, but I completely lost it. I snapped at her. I said, \"Martha, can't you see I'm doing the work of ten men here?\" It's just... they interrupt me with these tiny, insignificant things when I'm trying to accomplish something massive, and it just sets me right off. So, high mood? Absolutely. But irritable? Only because nobody else has the sense to keep up with the program!\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you noticed that you need much less sleep lately—like feeling rested after only a few hours of sleep—without feeling tired the next day?\n\nPatient: Well, sleep... sleep is an interesting topic right now, doctor. You see, I used to be a solid eight-hours-a-night man, absolutely had to have it, especially working the early shifts at the plant. You can't be nodding off when you're calibrating the grinding gear, the tolerances are just too tight. But lately? Honestly, I don't know what to tell you. I haven't really been paying attention to the clock at night. \n\nI mean, take last night. Martha went up to bed around, oh, maybe ten-thirty? She always watches that crime show and then goes up. I stayed down because I was right in the middle of re-evaluating the schedule for the church charity drive. I had all these spreadsheets spread out across the dining room table—which, by the way, is a logistical nightmare because half the table is covered in those green-labeled boxes for the den—and I just kept working. I was cross-referencing the volunteer availability with the donation drop-off points, and the next thing I knew, I looked out the window and the sun was coming up over the Henderson's roof across the street! \n\nBut the funny thing is, I didn't feel tired. Not even a little bit! I just made a pot of coffee—the strong stuff, French roast—and went straight out to the driveway to finish sorting the socket wrenches before I had to leave for my shift at the plant. So, do I need less sleep? I'm not really sure if I *need* less, or if I just don't have the time for it right now. There's just so much to do, and my mind is so sharp, I don't even feel the lack of it. I just keep going and going. I haven't really noticed feeling exhausted or anything like that the next day. I'm just... I'm just ready for the next task!\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself getting easily distracted by things around you, or have you been taking on more tasks or projects than you normally would, even if they seem unnecessary?\n\nPatient: Distracted? Well, doctor, I wouldn't say distracted so much as... highly attuned to my environment. My senses are just razor-sharp right now! But, yes, Martha has been harping on this exact thing, and I suppose if you look at it from the outside, I can see how she might think I'm taking on too much. \n\nIt’s like this—whether I’m down at the shop or working on the house, absolutely everything catches my attention. I’ll be right in the middle of a crucial task, say, adjusting the torque on the main grinding wheel at the plant, and then—*clank*—I hear a noise over by the loading dock. Or a delivery truck passes by outside, and I just have to go see if it's the new shipment of abrasive belts we ordered last Tuesday. And on the way to the loading dock, I’ll spot one tool out of place on Jimmy’s workbench—Jimmy never puts his calipers away right, I’ve told him a hundred times—so I stop to organize his station. But while I'm doing that, I realize the lighting over his bench is completely inadequate, so I head to the supply closet to find a higher wattage bulb. Just one little thing pulls me right off what I’m doing!\n\nAnd it's the exact same story at home with the packing. One minute I'm taping up those green-labeled boxes for the den, the next I hear the mail truck, so I go out to check the mail, notice the hinges on the mailbox are squeaking, head to the garage for the oil, and then I decide the whole garage workbench needs to be reorganized right that second based on those blueprints I was telling you about. Pretty soon, I’ve got five different things started—the packing, the mail, the hinges, the workbench, the lighting—and none of them are actually finished! Martha gets so frustrated, but I try to explain to her that they all need doing, every single one of them. It's just that my brain is moving so fast, I'm trying to tackle the whole world all at once!\n\nDoctor: Have you recently engaged in any activities that others might consider risky, impulsive, or out of character for you—like spending sprees, reckless driving, or making major decisions without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Risky? Impulsive? Well, doctor, that depends entirely on who you ask. If you ask Martha, she’ll tell you I’ve completely lost my sense of reason, but if you ask me, I call it being decisive. You see, I was telling you earlier about the garage at the new house and those blueprints I drew up. Well, you can't execute a professional-grade organizational system on a slanted concrete floor with a bunch of rusted-out pegboards. It’s impossible! So I went down to the supply center—and I also called up this contractor, Hank, who I actually took a couple of business management courses with at the community college back in the late seventies, good guy—and I just went ahead and put the deposits down. Right then and there! I put down money for garage work, a commercial air compressor, custom steel cabinetry, the whole nine yards. \n\nAnd yes, I did it without talking it over with my wife. But doctor, if I wait for Martha to sit down at the kitchen table, balance the checkbook, and deliberate over every single penny, that garage wouldn't be ready until next Christmas! I had to pull the trigger! The equipment was exactly what my setup needed.\n\nAnd because I’m trying to coordinate all this, I’ve been driving back and forth between the old house and the new house. Just back and forth, back and forth, probably six or seven times a day. Martha's sister rode with me once and practically left fingernail marks in the dashboard, but I'm just trying to get things done! And it’s the exact same story down at the plant. I’ve been running the blending vats and the grinders at absolute maximum capacity, pushing the mixing cycles through in record time because I know the machines can handle it. Dave, the floor manager, actually pulled me aside and told me I needed to slow down. He and a few others kept warning me, saying I was taking chances, that I was rushing the jobs and pushing the equipment too hard. \n\nSo, sure, people are warning me. They’re telling me I’m taking risks on the road, taking risks at work, spending money... but they just don’t get the big picture! When your mind is firing this fast and you can see exactly how everything needs to come together, you can't just sit around in the slow lane waiting for the rest of the world to catch up!\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Can you tell me exactly how long you've been feeling this way—this constant surge of energy, elevated mood, and increased activity?\n\nPatient: Exactly how long? Well, doctor, if you want the exact timeline, you have to look at what set this whole massive operation into motion. It all really kicked into high gear right when we were forced to start leaving the old house. We didn't want to move, you know. Thirty-two years in that house! My tomatoes in the back garden were legendary in the neighborhood. And then the county comes in with their zoning changes and eminent domain nonsense for the new highway bypass, and suddenly we are literally being forced out. Can you imagine the sheer audacity of it? Just uprooting a man's entire life! \n\nMartha was an absolute wreck about it, just paralyzed, sitting there with a roll of packing tape in her lap, crying and looking at the walls. But me? I realized right then and there that somebody had to take the bull by the horns. You can't just sit around and let the county dictate your momentum! So, that was... let me think, the final notices came in late summer, but the actual day we had to start physically leaving, getting our stuff out and dealing with the new property? That was... well, it's been about a month now. \n\nYes, a solid month. For four weeks, thirty days straight, I have just been riding this incredible wave of momentum. Ever since we were forced out and that very first box got packed a month ago, my mind just snapped to attention. I saw all the logistics, the garage blueprints, the scheduling for the plant, the church drive—everything just clicked into perfect clarity the minute we had to start leaving that house. So it's been about a month of just absolute, pure productivity, and honestly, I don't see any reason to slow down now!\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A week? Doctor, like I just told you, it’s been a solid month! Thirty days, day in and day out, without a single break in the action. And honestly, it’s been... well, I suppose it’s been a bit of a ride for the people around me. Because lately, I’ve been feeling like everybody needs to either get on board with my plans or just get completely out of my way! 

It’s the most incredible feeling, really. One minute I’m down at the plant or over at the new house, and I’m just feeling ten feet tall. I’m joking with the guys on the floor, I’m laughing, I’m feeling absolutely invincible because I can see exactly how everything is supposed to fit together. The energy is just spectacular, I feel like I could lift a Buick! But then—and this is what Martha keeps complaining about—then she or my boy will come up to me right in the middle of my momentum. 

My son came down to help with the move, you see, took time off work and everything, which is fine, but he just doesn't move at my pace. None of them do. So I'll be in this fantastic mood, feeling like a giant, and then Martha or my boy will ask me some tiny, insignificant little question. Something like, "Are you sure this box goes in the kitchen?" or "Do we really need the industrial compressor delivered today?" 

And I just snap. I mean, I go from joking around and feeling great to just biting their heads off over the absolute smallest thing! I can't help it! It's like, why are they questioning me when I clearly have the entire logistical operation mapped out in my head? If they would just listen to me and follow my lead instead of stopping the gears to ask these little questions, we'd have the whole house moved and the plant reorganized by Tuesday! So, yes, it's been a whole month of this nonstop drive—feeling on top of the world, but just... snapping at my wife or my boy the second they drag their feet. They just need to keep up!

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.13, F31.33, F31.43, F31.63, F31.73, F31.83, F31.93, F31.03, F31.23, F31.53\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I'm here because my family—specifically my daughter, she's the one who made the appointment—thinks I'm overdoing it. They say I've been doing too much, but honestly, it just feels like I'm the only one with enough drive to actually get things done around here. We're in the middle of this big move, you see, downsizing to a smaller place, and there are boxes everywhere. My wife just stares at them, but I've been up sorting, packing, labeling—color-coded labels, mind you, so we know exactly what goes where. \n\nAnd then there's the new house. The garage there is a disaster, absolute disaster. I've been drawing up blueprints to rebuild the whole shelving system because the previous owner had no idea what he was doing. I need a proper space for my tools, especially the grinding gear I sometimes bring home from the shop. \n\nOn top of all that, I’m organizing the annual charity drive at church. Pastor Jenkins is a good man, but he’s slow, you know? He needs someone to take the reins, so I’ve been calling the volunteers, setting up the schedules, mapping out the donation routes. And I'm still working full shifts at the plant. I’m a mixing and blending worker, have been for years, and these younger fellows they hire now, they just don't have the work ethic. I have to show them, step-by-step, how the job ought to be done, otherwise the whole batch gets ruined. So, yes, I'm busy, very busy, but someone has to do it, right? If I don't, it all falls apart. But my daughter, she just doesn't see it that way. She thinks I need to \"slow down.\" Slow down! Can you imagine? At 74, I've got more energy than I did at 40!\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of time where you felt unusually energetic, needed less sleep, or were more talkative than usual, lasting for several days or more?\n\nPatient: Well, energetic is one word for it, doctor, but it’s more like... it’s like my whole body is just humming, you know? Like I’ve got a motor in me that just won’t shut off. I mean, take yesterday for example. I tried, I really tried to sit down in the recliner—the brown leather one, my wife bought it for my birthday three years ago because she said I needed a place to relax—but I couldn't even make it through five minutes of the evening news. The anchor was just droning on about the weather, and all I could think about was the fact that the garage at the new place still hasn't been measured properly for those shelving blueprints I told you about. \n\nSo, I’m up out of the chair, pacing around, sorting through the boxes in the living room—the ones with the red labels, those are for the kitchen appliances, obviously—and then I’m out in the driveway loading my tools into the truck. I had my socket wrenches, the power drill, the grinding gear I brought from the plant—because you never know when you're going to need to smooth down a rough edge on a bracket—and before I know it, I’m driving back and forth between the old house and the new house. I just can't stop moving. It’s like if I stop, everything is going to pile up, and nobody else is going to handle it. My wife just watches me, shaking her head, but I tell her, \"Martha, the boxes aren't going to unpack themselves, and the shelves aren't going to build themselves.\" So, yes, the energy is there, constantly, pushing me from one task to the next, hour after hour, and I just don't have the time to sit still when there's so much left to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your mood was unusually high, euphoric, or irritable—where you felt like you were on top of the world or easily annoyed for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, \"top of the world\" is exactly how I'd put it. I mean, I wake up and I just feel... magnificent. Like I'm ten feet tall! I'll go into the plant and I'm joking with the guys on the floor, laughing, feeling like I could run the entire blending operation single-handed. But the problem—and this is what my daughter doesn't seem to understand—the problem isn't me, it's everybody else. \n\nI’ve just been feeling lately like everybody needs to either get on board with my plans or get completely out of my way. Because I have a vision for how things need to be done, whether it's the church donation routes or the packing system for the new house, and they all just drag their feet. So, yes, I suppose I do get annoyed, but I wouldn't say it's for \"no clear reason.\" I’ll go from joking around and feeling absolutely invincible to just snapping at my wife or my boy over some little question. \n\nLike yesterday, my son came over to help with the heavy boxes—the blue-labeled ones, obviously for the basement—and he actually asked me if we should just leave them in the hallway for now. The hallway! After I had just spent three hours drawing up a precise floor plan for the basement storage! Or Martha, she came out to the driveway and asked me what I wanted for lunch right when I was in the middle of calculating the load-bearing capacity for the new shelving brackets. Just a little question, I know, but I completely lost it. I snapped at her. I said, \"Martha, can't you see I'm doing the work of ten men here?\" It's just... they interrupt me with these tiny, insignificant things when I'm trying to accomplish something massive, and it just sets me right off. So, high mood? Absolutely. But irritable? Only because nobody else has the sense to keep up with the program!\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you noticed that you need much less sleep lately—like feeling rested after only a few hours of sleep—without feeling tired the next day?\n\nPatient: Well, sleep... sleep is an interesting topic right now, doctor. You see, I used to be a solid eight-hours-a-night man, absolutely had to have it, especially working the early shifts at the plant. You can't be nodding off when you're calibrating the grinding gear, the tolerances are just too tight. But lately? Honestly, I don't know what to tell you. I haven't really been paying attention to the clock at night. \n\nI mean, take last night. Martha went up to bed around, oh, maybe ten-thirty? She always watches that crime show and then goes up. I stayed down because I was right in the middle of re-evaluating the schedule for the church charity drive. I had all these spreadsheets spread out across the dining room table—which, by the way, is a logistical nightmare because half the table is covered in those green-labeled boxes for the den—and I just kept working. I was cross-referencing the volunteer availability with the donation drop-off points, and the next thing I knew, I looked out the window and the sun was coming up over the Henderson's roof across the street! \n\nBut the funny thing is, I didn't feel tired. Not even a little bit! I just made a pot of coffee—the strong stuff, French roast—and went straight out to the driveway to finish sorting the socket wrenches before I had to leave for my shift at the plant. So, do I need less sleep? I'm not really sure if I *need* less, or if I just don't have the time for it right now. There's just so much to do, and my mind is so sharp, I don't even feel the lack of it. I just keep going and going. I haven't really noticed feeling exhausted or anything like that the next day. I'm just... I'm just ready for the next task!\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself getting easily distracted by things around you, or have you been taking on more tasks or projects than you normally would, even if they seem unnecessary?\n\nPatient: Distracted? Well, doctor, I wouldn't say distracted so much as... highly attuned to my environment. My senses are just razor-sharp right now! But, yes, Martha has been harping on this exact thing, and I suppose if you look at it from the outside, I can see how she might think I'm taking on too much. \n\nIt’s like this—whether I’m down at the shop or working on the house, absolutely everything catches my attention. I’ll be right in the middle of a crucial task, say, adjusting the torque on the main grinding wheel at the plant, and then—*clank*—I hear a noise over by the loading dock. Or a delivery truck passes by outside, and I just have to go see if it's the new shipment of abrasive belts we ordered last Tuesday. And on the way to the loading dock, I’ll spot one tool out of place on Jimmy’s workbench—Jimmy never puts his calipers away right, I’ve told him a hundred times—so I stop to organize his station. But while I'm doing that, I realize the lighting over his bench is completely inadequate, so I head to the supply closet to find a higher wattage bulb. Just one little thing pulls me right off what I’m doing!\n\nAnd it's the exact same story at home with the packing. One minute I'm taping up those green-labeled boxes for the den, the next I hear the mail truck, so I go out to check the mail, notice the hinges on the mailbox are squeaking, head to the garage for the oil, and then I decide the whole garage workbench needs to be reorganized right that second based on those blueprints I was telling you about. Pretty soon, I’ve got five different things started—the packing, the mail, the hinges, the workbench, the lighting—and none of them are actually finished! Martha gets so frustrated, but I try to explain to her that they all need doing, every single one of them. It's just that my brain is moving so fast, I'm trying to tackle the whole world all at once!\n\nDoctor: Have you recently engaged in any activities that others might consider risky, impulsive, or out of character for you—like spending sprees, reckless driving, or making major decisions without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Risky? Impulsive? Well, doctor, that depends entirely on who you ask. If you ask Martha, she’ll tell you I’ve completely lost my sense of reason, but if you ask me, I call it being decisive. You see, I was telling you earlier about the garage at the new house and those blueprints I drew up. Well, you can't execute a professional-grade organizational system on a slanted concrete floor with a bunch of rusted-out pegboards. It’s impossible! So I went down to the supply center—and I also called up this contractor, Hank, who I actually took a couple of business management courses with at the community college back in the late seventies, good guy—and I just went ahead and put the deposits down. Right then and there! I put down money for garage work, a commercial air compressor, custom steel cabinetry, the whole nine yards. \n\nAnd yes, I did it without talking it over with my wife. But doctor, if I wait for Martha to sit down at the kitchen table, balance the checkbook, and deliberate over every single penny, that garage wouldn't be ready until next Christmas! I had to pull the trigger! The equipment was exactly what my setup needed.\n\nAnd because I’m trying to coordinate all this, I’ve been driving back and forth between the old house and the new house. Just back and forth, back and forth, probably six or seven times a day. Martha's sister rode with me once and practically left fingernail marks in the dashboard, but I'm just trying to get things done! And it’s the exact same story down at the plant. I’ve been running the blending vats and the grinders at absolute maximum capacity, pushing the mixing cycles through in record time because I know the machines can handle it. Dave, the floor manager, actually pulled me aside and told me I needed to slow down. He and a few others kept warning me, saying I was taking chances, that I was rushing the jobs and pushing the equipment too hard. \n\nSo, sure, people are warning me. They’re telling me I’m taking risks on the road, taking risks at work, spending money... but they just don’t get the big picture! When your mind is firing this fast and you can see exactly how everything needs to come together, you can't just sit around in the slow lane waiting for the rest of the world to catch up!\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Can you tell me exactly how long you've been feeling this way—this constant surge of energy, elevated mood, and increased activity?\n\nPatient: Exactly how long? Well, doctor, if you want the exact timeline, you have to look at what set this whole massive operation into motion. It all really kicked into high gear right when we were forced to start leaving the old house. We didn't want to move, you know. Thirty-two years in that house! My tomatoes in the back garden were legendary in the neighborhood. And then the county comes in with their zoning changes and eminent domain nonsense for the new highway bypass, and suddenly we are literally being forced out. Can you imagine the sheer audacity of it? Just uprooting a man's entire life! \n\nMartha was an absolute wreck about it, just paralyzed, sitting there with a roll of packing tape in her lap, crying and looking at the walls. But me? I realized right then and there that somebody had to take the bull by the horns. You can't just sit around and let the county dictate your momentum! So, that was... let me think, the final notices came in late summer, but the actual day we had to start physically leaving, getting our stuff out and dealing with the new property? That was... well, it's been about a month now. \n\nYes, a solid month. For four weeks, thirty days straight, I have just been riding this incredible wave of momentum. Ever since we were forced out and that very first box got packed a month ago, my mind just snapped to attention. I saw all the logistics, the garage blueprints, the scheduling for the plant, the church drive—everything just clicked into perfect clarity the minute we had to start leaving that house. So it's been about a month of just absolute, pure productivity, and honestly, I don't see any reason to slow down now!\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period where you felt this way — this intense energy, elevated mood, and constant drive — without any break, for at least a full week at a time?\n\nPatient: A week? Doctor, like I just told you, it’s been a solid month! Thirty days, day in and day out, without a single break in the action. And honestly, it’s been... well, I suppose it’s been a bit of a ride for the people around me. Because lately, I’ve been feeling like everybody needs to either get on board with my plans or just get completely out of my way! \n\nIt’s the most incredible feeling, really. One minute I’m down at the plant or over at the new house, and I’m just feeling ten feet tall. I’m joking with the guys on the floor, I’m laughing, I’m feeling absolutely invincible because I can see exactly how everything is supposed to fit together. I mean, I go from joking around and feeling great to just biting their heads off over the absolute smallest thing! I can't help it! One minute I'm taping up those green-labeled boxes for the den, the next I hear the mail truck, so I go out to check the mail, notice the hinges on the mailbox are squeaking, head to the garage for the oil, and then I decide the whole garage workbench needs to be reorganized right that second based on those blueprints I was telling you about. Pretty soon, I’ve got five different things started—the packing, the mail, the hinges, the workbench, the lighting—and none of them are actually finished! Martha gets so frustrated, but I try to explain to her that they all need doing, every single one of them. It's just that my brain is moving so fast, I'm trying to tackle the whole world all at once!\n\nDoctor: Have you recently engaged in any activities that others might consider risky, impulsive, or out of character for you—like spending sprees, reckless driving, or making major decisions without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Risky? Impulsive? Well, doctor, that depends entirely on who you ask. If you ask Martha, she’ll tell you I’ve completely lost my sense of reason, but if you ask me, I call it being decisive. You see, I was telling you earlier about the garage at the new house and those blueprints I drew up. Well, you can't execute a professional-grade organizational system on a slanted concrete floor with a bunch of rusted-out pegboards. It’s impossible! So I went down to the supply center—and I also called up this contractor, Hank, who I actually took a couple of business management courses with at the community college back in the late seventies, good guy—and I just went ahead and put the deposits down. Right then and there! I put down money for garage work, a commercial air compressor, custom steel cabinetry, the whole nine yards. \n\nAnd yes, I did it without talking it over with my wife. But doctor, if I wait for Martha to sit down at the kitchen table, balance the checkbook, and deliberate over every single penny, that garage wouldn't be ready until next Christmas! I had to pull the trigger! The equipment was exactly what my setup needed.\n\nAnd because I’m trying to coordinate all this, I’ve been driving back and forth between the old house and the new house. Just back and forth, back and forth, probably six or seven times a day. Martha's sister rode with me once and practically left fingernail marks in the dashboard, but I'm just trying to get things done! And it’s the exact same story down at the plant. I’ve been running the blending vats and the grinders at absolute maximum capacity, pushing the mixing cycles through in record time because I know the machines can handle it. Dave, the floor manager, actually pulled me aside and told me I needed to slow down. He and a few others kept warning me, saying I was taking chances, that I was rushing the jobs and pushing the equipment too hard. \n\nSo, sure, people are warning me. They’re telling me I’m taking risks on the road, taking risks at work, spending money... but they just don’t get the big picture! When your mind is firing this fast and you can see exactly how everything needs to come together, you can't just sit around in the slow lane waiting for the rest of the world to catch up!\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Can you tell me exactly how long you've been feeling this way—this constant surge of energy, elevated mood, and increased activity?\n\nPatient: Exactly how long? Well, doctor, if you want the exact timeline, you have to look at what set this whole massive operation into motion. It all really kicked into high gear right when we were forced to start leaving the old house. We didn't want to move, you know. Thirty-two years in that house! My tomatoes in the back garden were legendary in the neighborhood. And then the county comes in with their zoning changes and eminent domain nonsense for the new highway bypass, and suddenly we are literally being forced out. Can you imagine the sheer audacity of it? Just uprooting a man's entire life! \n\nMartha was an absolute wreck about it, just paralyzed, sitting there with a roll of packing tape in her lap, crying and looking at the walls. But me? I realized right then and there that somebody had to take the bull by the horns. You can't just sit around and let the county dictate your momentum! So, that was... let me think, the final notices came in late summer, but the actual day we had to start physically leaving, getting our stuff out and dealing with the new property? That was... well, it's been about a month now. \n\nYes, a solid month. For four weeks, thirty days straight, I have just been riding this incredible wave of momentum. Ever since we were forced out and that very first box got packed a month ago, my mind just snapped to attention. I saw all the logistics, the garage blueprints, the scheduling for the plant, the church drive—everything just clicked into perfect clarity the minute we had to start leaving that house. So it's been about a month of just absolute, pure productivity, and honestly, I don't see any reason to slow down now!\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period where you felt this way — this intense energy, elevated mood, and constant drive — without any break, for at least a full week at a time?\n\nPatient: A week? Doctor, like I just told you, it’s been a solid month! Thirty days, day in and day out, without a single break in the action. Because lately, I’ve been feeling like everybody needs to either get on board with my plans or just get completely out of my way! \n\nIt’s the most incredible feeling, really. One minute I’m down at the plant or over at the new house, and I’m just feeling ten feet tall. I’m joking with the guys on the floor, I’m laughing, I’m feeling absolutely invincible because I can see exactly how everything is supposed to fit together. The energy is just spectacular, I feel like I could lift a Buick! ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, doctor—it’s exactly that. And I can tell you right now, it is definitely not just stress from the move or the plant. Look, I’ve been running industrial grinders and blending vats for decades. I know what stress is. Stress makes a man tired. Stress makes you want to come home, kick off your steel-toe boots, and sink into the recliner with a cold drink. I don't want to sit down! 

This is entirely different from my usual self. I’m usually a very methodical, steady guy. Ask anyone down at the facility. But lately, like I was just telling you, I’ve been feeling like everybody needs to either get on board with my plans or get completely out of my way! It’s this wild swing that even I can see is different. I’ll be having the time of my life, joking with the guys on the floor, feeling ten feet tall and absolutely brilliant because everything is running so smoothly in my head. And then—bam!—the second my wife or my boy interrupts me with some little question about where a lamp goes or why I bought a certain tool, I am snapping at them like a bear in a trap. 

I mean, just biting their heads off over nothing! Martha actually looked at me the other day in the kitchen—well, the kitchen of the new place, which is still a disaster area, by the way—and she said, "Who are you right now?" Because I'm usually the guy who would sit down, grab a pen, and draw a little diagram on a napkin to explain my reasoning to her. Now? I have zero patience for it. Zero! So no, it’s not stress. It’s just that my brain is operating on a completely different, higher level right now, and going from feeling that invincible high to dealing with their constant foot-dragging... well, it makes me snap.
