Doc, I really feel like you're not listening to me. Didn't we literally just go over this? I mean, whether you're talking about right before the county notices or thirty years ago, the answer is still exactly the same. No. I'm seventy-four years old, and I can tell you with absolute certainty that I have never, in all my decades on this earth, experienced anything remotely like what I'm going through right now. 

I mean, let's look at my history, right? I took some college classes way back when—thought about doing something in administration or maybe even engineering—but I ended up working the crushing and blending vats. And I stayed there. For thirty-five years, I was a steady, reliable guy. Did I have good days? Sure! When Martha and I finally paid off the mortgage on our first place, I was on top of the world. I probably had a spring in my step for a whole month! Or when my boy was born, I stayed up for two days straight just handing out cigars and grinning like a fool. But that was just... normal life stuff, you know? It was just a regular guy being happy about regular things. 

It was absolutely nothing like this current situation. I didn't have this... this endless well of energy where I don't even need to sleep because my brain is just solving complex logistical problems in the dark! I've never had a time in my past where I'd snap at Martha just for asking what's for dinner because her question interrupted a master plan I was forming. 

So no, not in my twenties, not in my fifties, not ever. This is completely unprecedented, doctor. It's like I've been asleep my whole life and I just finally woke up. So I really don't understand why we keep digging around in the past when what's happening right now is so spectacular! If you really want to understand what's going on with me, we should be focusing on the present. We should be talking about the new blueprints, because the way I've figured out how to optimize the square footage is just... well, it's revolutionary, and I haven't even gotten to show you the sketches I made on the napkins in the waiting room!

They just need to keep up!\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you had a period where your mood was so high or irritable that it was clearly different from your usual self, not just a response to the stress of moving or managing the plant?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, doctor—it’s exactly that. And I can tell you right now, it is definitely not just stress from the move or the plant. Look, I’ve been running industrial grinders and blending vats for decades. I know what stress is. Stress makes a man tired. Stress makes you want to come home, kick off your steel-toe boots, and sink into the recliner with a cold drink. I don't want to sit down! \n\nThis is entirely different from my usual self. I’m usually a very methodical, steady guy. Ask anyone down at the facility. But lately, like I was just telling you, I’ve been feeling like everybody needs to either get on board with my plans or get completely out of my way! It’s this wild swing that even I can see is different. I’ll be having the time of my life, joking with the guys on the floor, feeling ten feet tall and absolutely brilliant because everything is running so smoothly in my head. And then—bam!—the second my wife or my boy interrupts me with some little question about where a lamp goes or why I bought a certain tool, I am snapping at them like a bear in a trap. \n\nI mean, just biting their heads off over nothing! Martha actually looked at me the other day in the kitchen—well, the kitchen of the new place, which is still a disaster area, by the way—and she said, \"Who are you right now?\" Because I'm usually the guy who would sit down, grab a pen, and draw a little diagram on a napkin to explain my reasoning to her. Now? I have zero patience for it. Zero! So no, it’s not stress. It’s just that my brain is operating on a completely different, higher level right now, and going from feeling that invincible high to dealing with their constant foot-dragging... well, it makes me snap.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days recently where you felt down or hopeless for most of the day, even when things were going well?\n\nPatient: Down? Hopeless? Doctor, are you even listening to what I’ve been telling you? No, absolutely not! Not for a single minute, let alone a whole day. I mean, why in the world would I feel down right now? I just spent the last twenty minutes explaining how I feel like I can lift a Buick! \n\nI haven't had a single moment of feeling hopeless. Hopeless is what Martha was feeling when she was sitting there with that packing tape, staring at the walls of the old house. Hopeless is when you let the county dictate your life. But me? I am the exact opposite of hopeless right now. I am entirely, one hundred percent full of hope, and drive, and vision! \n\nI mean, just yesterday, the guys down at the plant were telling me we had a backup on line three—the main blending vat was seizing up. Now, a month ago, maybe I would have sighed, rubbed my temples, and called maintenance. But yesterday? I just laughed! I marched right down there, I saw exactly what the torque issue was in my head before I even took the casing off, and I had them running again in twenty minutes. It was brilliant! I felt like I was conducting a symphony of gears and rotors! \n\nSo, no, I haven't felt down or sad or whatever you're asking about. Not at all. I don't even have time to feel down! Between the plant, moving into the new house, getting the garage blueprints finalized, and running the church drive, there simply aren't enough hours in the day to sit around feeling hopeless. I am firing on all cylinders, doctor. Every single one of them!\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts have been racing so fast that you could barely keep up with them, or that one idea quickly jumps to another in your mind?\n\nPatient: Racing? No, I wouldn't call it racing. That makes it sound like things are out of control, like I'm overwhelmed or confused, and I am absolutely not confused. If anything, my mind is just incredibly... efficient right now. It's not that the thoughts are moving too fast to keep up with, it's that I'm finally seeing how everything connects! \n\nI mean, take the garage blueprints, for example. I was looking at the electrical layout the other night—well, it was probably 3:00 AM, but who's counting, right? I was looking at the wiring diagram for the new 220-volt outlets I need for my heavy equipment. Now, normally, I'd just look at that one thing. But right then, I instantly realized that the conduit path for the 220 line perfectly mirrored the new ventilation system we need down at the plant for the blending vats! It was like a flash of pure clarity. So I started sketching out the ventilation changes on the back of the garage blueprints, and while I was doing that, it hit me that the airflow dynamics were exactly the same principle I was trying to explain to the committee for the church drive about how we need to route traffic for the upcoming bake sale! \n\nSo, you see, it's not that I'm jumping from one thing to another in a disorganized way. It's that everything is connected! The garage, the plant, the church—it's all one big logistical system, and I am the only one who can see the whole board right now. I don't feel like I can't keep up. I feel like everyone else is moving in slow motion! Martha says I'm talking too fast, that I'm jumping around, but I keep telling her, \"Martha, if you'd just pay attention, you'd see that the bake sale traffic flow is directly related to the 220-volt line in the garage!\" She just shakes her head. But no, my thoughts aren't racing out of control. They are sharp, doctor. Razor-sharp.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods recently where your mood was so unusually high, expansive, or irritable that it was clearly different from your usual self, even when things weren’t particularly stressful or exciting?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I feel like we’re going in circles here! Didn’t I just answer this a few minutes ago? I mean, I don't mind repeating myself if you need to write it down in your file, but yes, absolutely. Like I was saying right at the start of all this, I have definitely been having these periods where my mood is just... well, it's huge. \n\nI’ve been feeling like everybody needs to either get on board with my plans or get completely out of my way! It’s exactly like I told you. I’ll be down at the plant, or even just in the new kitchen, and I'll be joking around with the guys, feeling ten feet tall, just on top of the world because my mind is firing so beautifully. Everything is perfectly clear to me. And then—bam!—Martha or my boy will come up to me and interrupt my flow with some completely insignificant, tiny little question. \"Where does this box go?\" or \"Why are you drawing on the blueprints?\" And I just go from laughing and feeling invincible to snapping at them in a split second. \n\nI mean, I will just bite their heads off over absolutely nothing! It’s just that I’m moving at this incredible speed, and they’re all dragging their feet, and it makes me incredibly irritable when they can't keep up with what I'm trying to build here. So, yes, doc, I’ve definitely had that, just like I said before. Are you getting all this down now?\n\nDoctor: Before all of this moving stress started—say, before the county notices came in—have you ever had a period of time lasting at least four days where you felt this same kind of high energy, elevated mood, or irritability?\n\nPatient: Four days? Before the move? Well, let me think back. I mean, the county notices started coming in around late spring, and that's when things really kicked into high gear with the new property and all the logistics. Before that... well, honestly, I don't recall anything quite like this. \n\nI mean, sure, I've always been a hard worker. You don't spend thirty-five years working the blending vats without learning how to put your shoulder to the wheel. There were times when we had big orders to fill, and I'd work double shifts, fueled on nothing but coffee and the satisfaction of a job well done. But that was just work, you know? That was just me being a dedicated employee. It wasn't this... this magnificent clarity I have right now. It wasn't this feeling that I could redesign the entire factory floor in my sleep. \n\nAnd the irritability? Well, I won't lie, I've never been a patient man when people do things half-assed. If a new guy on the line didn't secure the safety latch on the grinding drum, I'd give him a piece of my mind, sure. But that was just common sense. It wasn't like now, where just the sound of someone breathing too slowly makes me want to scream! \n\nSo, no, before all this business with the house and the move... I can't say I ever had a stretch of four days or whatever where I felt exactly like this. This is new. This is... this is an awakening, doctor! It's like I've been operating on a six-volt battery my whole life, and someone just hooked me up to a high-tension power line! Before the spring, I was just regular old me. Good at my job, sure, but not... not this. Not firing on all cylinders like a finely tuned machine. So, no, nothing like this before the county got involved.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other periods in your life, before this current one, where you felt unusually energetic, irritable, or on top of the world for several days or more?\n\nPatient: Doc, I really feel like you're not listening to me. Didn't we literally just go over this? I mean, whether you're talking about right before the county notices or thirty years ago, the answer is still exactly the same. No. I'm seventy-four years old, and I can tell you with absolute certainty that I have never, in all my decades on this earth, experienced anything remotely like what I'm going through right now. \n\nI mean, let's look at my history, right? I took some college classes way back when—thought about doing something in administration or maybe even engineering—but I ended up working the crushing and blending vats. And I stayed there. For thirty-five years, I was a steady, reliable guy. Did I have good days? Sure! When Martha and I finally paid off the mortgage on our first place, I was on top of the world. I probably had a spring in my step for a whole month! Or when my boy was born, I stayed up for two days straight just handing out cigars and grinning like a fool. But that was just... normal life stuff, you know? It was just a regular guy being happy about regular things. \n\nIt was absolutely nothing like this current situation. I didn't have this... this endless well of energy where I don't even need to sleep because my brain is just solving complex logistical problems in the dark! I've never had a time in my past where I'd snap at Martha just for asking what's for dinner because her question interrupted a master plan I was forming. \n\nSo no, not in my twenties, not in my fifties, not ever. This is completely unprecedented, doctor. It's like I've been asleep my whole life and I just finally woke up. So I really don't understand why we keep digging around in the past when what's happening right now is so spectacular! If you really want to understand what's going on with me, we should be focusing on the present. We should be talking about the new blueprints, because the way I've figured out how to optimize the square footage is just... well, it's revolutionary, and I haven't even gotten to show you the sketches I made on the napkins in the waiting room!\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doctor, are you... is there something wrong with your hearing? Or is this some kind of psychological test to see how many times a man can answer the exact same question before he explodes? Because I am telling you—read my lips here—NO. Capital N, capital O. Never!

This is the first time! The very first time! I am seventy-four years old and I have finally tapped into the main grid, and you keep trying to talk about the days when I was running on a dying flashlight battery! 

Now, I am begging you, can we please move on? Because you are wasting incredibly valuable time. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... I am honestly stunned. I am literally speechless, which, if you ask Martha, is a medical miracle in itself! Are you... are you serious right now? Are you actually asking me this again? What is happening here? Is there a hidden camera? Is this some kind of stress test for my heart? Because if it is, you are doing a fantastic job, doc, truly top-notch!

Look, I am a reasonable man. I worked thirty-five years at the vats, you don't survive that kind of industrial environment without being able to follow instructions and communicate clearly. But I feel like I am speaking a foreign language here! I have told you, I have explained it sideways, backwards, and forwards: No! Never! Nada! Zilch! 

There has never been a time—not when I was taking those college courses, not when I started at the plant, not when I retired—never a time where I felt like this just out of the blue. If I was happy, it was because of a reason. If I was mad, it was because of a reason. I was a completely normal, average, run-of-the-mill guy who never once experienced this absolute supernova of brain power until right now! 

I mean, I'm trying to be patient here, I really am, but my mind is moving at a million miles an hour and we are stuck in this endless loop of you asking the same question over and over and over! It's like a skipping record! And meanwhile, the real issue—the actual genius of what I am trying to explain to you about the load-bearing distribution on the new property—is just sitting here in my pocket! I have the math all figured out! I did it in my head while you were asking me that question for the fourth time! So please, I am begging you, as a fellow man of science, can we please, for the love of all that is holy, talk about something else?!

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.13, F31.33, F31.43, F31.63, F31.73, F31.83, F31.93, F31.03, F31.23, F31.53\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I'm here because my family—specifically my daughter, she's the one who made the appointment—thinks I'm overdoing it. They say I've been doing too much, but honestly, it just feels like I'm the only one with enough drive to actually get things done around here. We're in the middle of this big move, you see, downsizing to a smaller place, and there are boxes everywhere. My wife just stares at them, but I've been up sorting, packing, labeling—color-coded labels, mind you, so we know exactly what goes where. \n\nAnd then there's the new house. The garage there is a disaster, absolute disaster. I've been drawing up blueprints to rebuild the whole shelving system because the previous owner had no idea what he was doing. I need a proper space for my tools, especially the grinding gear I sometimes bring home from the shop. \n\nOn top of all that, I’m organizing the annual charity drive at church. Pastor Jenkins is a good man, but he’s slow, you know? He needs someone to take the reins, so I’ve been calling the volunteers, setting up the schedules, mapping out the donation routes. And I'm still working full shifts at the plant. I’m a mixing and blending worker, have been for years, and these younger fellows they hire now, they just don't have the work ethic. I have to show them, step-by-step, how the job ought to be done, otherwise the whole batch gets ruined. So, yes, I'm busy, very busy, but someone has to do it, right? If I don't, it all falls apart. But my daughter, she just doesn't see it that way. She thinks I need to \"slow down.\" Slow down! Can you imagine? At 74, I've got more energy than I did at 40!\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of time where you felt unusually energetic, needed less sleep, or were more talkative than usual, lasting for several days or more?\n\nPatient: Well, energetic is one word for it, doctor, but it’s more like... it’s like my whole body is just humming, you know? Like I’ve got a motor in me that just won’t shut off. I mean, take yesterday for example. I tried, I really tried to sit down in the recliner—the brown leather one, my wife bought it for my birthday three years ago because she said I needed a place to relax—but I couldn't even make it through five minutes of the evening news. The anchor was just droning on about the weather, and all I could think about was the fact that the garage at the new place still hasn't been measured properly for those shelving blueprints I told you about. \n\nSo, I’m up out of the chair, pacing around, sorting through the boxes in the living room—the ones with the red labels, those are for the kitchen appliances, obviously—and then I’m out in the driveway loading my tools into the truck. I had my socket wrenches, the power drill, the grinding gear I brought from the plant—because you never know when you're going to need to smooth down a rough edge on a bracket—and before I know it, I’m driving back and forth between the old house and the new house. I just can't stop moving. It’s like if I stop, everything is going to pile up, and nobody else is going to handle it. My wife just watches me, shaking her head, but I tell her, \"Martha, the boxes aren't going to unpack themselves, and the shelves aren't going to build themselves.\" So, yes, the energy is there, constantly, pushing me from one task to the next, hour after hour, and I just don't have the time to sit still when there's so much left to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your mood was unusually high, euphoric, or irritable—where you felt like you were on top of the world or easily annoyed for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, \"top of the world\" is exactly how I'd put it. I mean, I wake up and I just feel... magnificent. Like I'm ten feet tall! I'll go into the plant and I'm joking with the guys on the floor, laughing, feeling like I could run the entire blending operation single-handed. But the problem—and this is what my daughter doesn't seem to understand—the problem isn't me, it's everybody else. \n\nI’ve just been feeling lately like everybody needs to either get on board with my plans or get completely out of my way. Because I have a vision for how things need to be done, whether it's the church donation routes or the packing system for the new house, and they all just drag their feet. So, yes, I suppose I do get annoyed, but I wouldn't say it's for \"no clear reason.\" I’ll go from joking around and feeling absolutely invincible to just snapping at my wife or my boy over some little question. \n\nLike yesterday, my son came over to help with the heavy boxes—the blue-labeled ones, obviously for the basement—and he actually asked me if we should just leave them in the hallway for now. The hallway! After I had just spent three hours drawing up a precise floor plan for the basement storage! Or Martha, she came out to the driveway and asked me what I wanted for lunch right when I was in the middle of calculating the load-bearing capacity for the new shelving brackets. Just a little question, I know, but I completely lost it. I snapped at her. I said, \"Martha, can't you see I'm doing the work of ten men here?\" It's just... they interrupt me with these tiny, insignificant things when I'm trying to accomplish something massive, and it just sets me right off. So, high mood? Absolutely. But irritable? Only because nobody else has the sense to keep up with the program!\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you noticed that you need much less sleep lately—like feeling rested after only a few hours of sleep—without feeling tired the next day?\n\nPatient: Well, sleep... sleep is an interesting topic right now, doctor. You see, I used to be a solid eight-hours-a-night man, absolutely had to have it, especially working the early shifts at the plant. You can't be nodding off when you're calibrating the grinding gear, the tolerances are just too tight. But lately? Honestly, I don't know what to tell you. I haven't really been paying attention to the clock at night. \n\nI mean, take last night. Martha went up to bed around, oh, maybe ten-thirty? She always watches that crime show and then goes up. I stayed down because I was right in the middle of re-evaluating the schedule for the church charity drive. I had all these spreadsheets spread out across the dining room table—which, by the way, is a logistical nightmare because half the table is covered in those green-labeled boxes for the den—and I just kept working. I was cross-referencing the volunteer availability with the donation drop-off points, and the next thing I knew, I looked out the window and the sun was coming up over the Henderson's roof across the street! \n\nBut the funny thing is, I didn't feel tired. Not even a little bit! I just made a pot of coffee—the strong stuff, French roast—and went straight out to the driveway to finish sorting the socket wrenches before I had to leave for my shift at the plant. So, do I need less sleep? I'm not really sure if I *need* less, or if I just don't have the time for it right now. There's just so much to do, and my mind is so sharp, I don't even feel the lack of it. I just keep going and going. I haven't really noticed feeling exhausted or anything like that the next day. I'm just... I'm just ready for the next task!\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself getting easily distracted by things around you, or have you been taking on more tasks or projects than you normally would, even if they seem unnecessary?\n\nPatient: Distracted? Well, doctor, I wouldn't say distracted so much as... highly attuned to my environment. My senses are just razor-sharp right now! But, yes, Martha has been harping on this exact thing, and I suppose if you look at it from the outside, I can see how she might think I'm taking on too much. \n\nIt’s like this—whether I’m down at the shop or working on the house, absolutely everything catches my attention. I’ll be right in the middle of a crucial task, say, adjusting the torque on the main grinding wheel at the plant, and then—*clank*—I hear a noise over by the loading dock. Or a delivery truck passes by outside, and I just have to go see if it's the new shipment of abrasive belts we ordered last Tuesday. And on the way to the loading dock, I’ll spot one tool out of place on Jimmy’s workbench—Jimmy never puts his calipers away right, I’ve told him a hundred times—so I stop to organize his station. But while I'm doing that, I realize the lighting over his bench is completely inadequate, so I head to the supply closet to find a higher wattage bulb. Just one little thing pulls me right off what I’m doing!\n\nAnd it's the exact same story at home with the packing. I was cross-referencing the volunteer availability with the donation drop-off points, and the next thing I knew, I looked out the window and the sun was coming up over the Henderson's roof across the street! \n\nBut the funny thing is, I didn't feel tired. There's just so much to do, and my mind is so sharp, I don't even feel the lack of it. I just keep going and going. I haven't really noticed feeling exhausted or anything like that the next day.
